The **Indian National Congress** (**INC**), also known as the **Congress Party**, or simply the **Congress**, is a [big tent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent)[^cite_note-33] [political party in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_parties_in_India). It is India's oldest political party and is widely regarded as one of the world's oldest continuously active political parties, outside [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_political_party) and [North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_parties_in_the_United_States). Founded on 28 December 1885, it was the first modern [nationalist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) to emerge in the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) in Asia and Africa.[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-Marshall2001-34] From the late 19th century, and especially after 1920, under the leadership of [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi), the Congress became the principal leader of the [Indian independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement).[^cite_note-research-36] The Congress was one of the parties that led India to independence from the [United Kingdom](31717.md),[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-Chiriyankandath2016-37][^cite_note-40][^cite_note-KopsteinLichbach2014-39] and significantly influenced other [anti-colonial nationalist movements](302084.md) in the British Empire.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-Marshall2001-34]

The INC is a "[big tent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent)" party that has been described as sitting on the [centre](39619035.md) of the [Indian political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_politics) spectrum.[^cite_note-Barrington2009-15][^cite_note-centrist-21][^cite_note-British-Journal-42] The party held its first session in 1885 in [Bombay](19189.md) where [W. C. Bannerjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Womesh_Chunder_Bonnerjee) presided over it.[^cite_note-43] After Indian independence in 1947, Congress emerged as a [catch-all](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catch-all_party), [Indian nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_nationalism) and [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_in_India) party, dominating Indian politics for the next 50 years. The party's first prime minister, [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru), led the Congress to support [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist) policies by creating the [Planning Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planning_Commission_\(India\)), introducing [Five-Year Plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-Year_Plans_of_India), implementing a mixed economy, and establishing a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state). After Nehru's death and the short tenure of [Lal Bahadur Shastri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lal_Bahadur_Shastri), Indira Gandhi became the leader of the party. In the 17 general elections since independence, it has won an outright majority on seven occasions and has led the ruling coalition a further three times, heading the central government for more than 54 years. There have been six prime ministers from the Congress party, the first being Jawaharlal Nehru (1947–1964), and the most recent being [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) (2004–2014). Since the 1990s, the [Bharatiya Janata Party](149330.md) has emerged as the main rival of the Congress in both national and regional politics.

In 1969, the party suffered a major split, with a faction led by [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) leaving to form the [Congress (R)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Congress_\(R\)), with the remainder becoming the [Congress (O)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Congress_\(Organisation\)). The Congress (R) became the dominant faction, winning the [1971 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Indian_general_election) by a huge margin. From 1975 to 1977, Indira Gandhi declared a [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Emergency_\(India\)) in India, resulting in widespread oppression and abuses of power. Another split in the party occurred in 1979, leading to the creation of the Congress (I), which was recognised as the Congress by the [Election Commission](47863083.md) in 1981. Under [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi)'s leadership, the party won a massive victory in the [1984 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Indian_general_election), nevertheless losing the election held in 1989 to the [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(India\)). The Congress then returned to power under [P. V. Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao), who moved the party towards an [economically liberal agenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India), a sharp break from previous leaders. However, it lost the [1996 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Indian_general_election) and was replaced in government by the National Front. After a record eight years out of office, the Congress-led coalition known as the [United Progressive Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance) (UPA) under Manmohan Singh formed a government after the [2004 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indian_general_election). Subsequently, the UPA again formed the government after winning the [2009 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Indian_general_election), and Singh became the first prime minister since [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) in 1971 to be re-elected after completing a full five-year term. However, under the leadership of [Rahul Gandhi](305010.md) in the [2014 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indian_general_election), the Congress suffered a heavy defeat, winning only 44 seats of the 543-member [Lok Sabha](275456.md) (the [lower house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_house) of the [Parliament of India](410238.md)). In the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indian_general_election), the party failed to make any substantial gains and won 52 seats, failing to form the [official opposition](3379016.md) yet again. In the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indian_general_election), the party performed better-than-expected, and won 99 seats, forming the official opposition with their highest seat count in a decade.[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45]

On social issues, it advocates secular policies that encourage [equal opportunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_opportunity), [right to health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_health), [right to education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_education), [civil liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberty), and support [social market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_market_economy), and a strong [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state). Being a centrist party, its policies predominantly reflected balanced positions including [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism), [egalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism), and [social stratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stratification). The INC supports contemporary economic reforms such as [liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization), [privatisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatization_in_India) and [globalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_globalisation). A total of 61 people have served as the president of the INC since its formation. [Sonia Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Gandhi) is the longest-serving president of the party, having held office for over twenty years from 1998 to 2017 and again from 2019 to 2022 (as interim). [Mallikarjun Kharge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mallikarjun_Kharge) is the current party [president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Indian_National_Congress). The district party is the smallest functional unit of Congress. There is also a [Pradesh Congress Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pradesh_Congress_Committee) (PCC), present at the state level in every state. Together, the delegates from the districts and PCCs form the [All India Congress Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Congress_Committee) (AICC). The party is additionally structured into various committees and segments including the [Working Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_Working_Committee) (CWC), [Seva Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seva_Dal), [Indian Youth Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Youth_Congress) (IYC), [Indian National Trade Union Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Trade_Union_Congress) (INTUC), and [National Students' Union of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Students'_Union_of_India) (NSUI).

## History

Main article: [History of the Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Indian_National_Congress)

### Foundation

See also: [Indian independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement)

During the latter part of the 1870s, there were concerted efforts among Indians to establish a [pan-Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) organisation for nationalist political influence.[^cite_note-Gehlot_1991_35-46] In 1883, [Allan Octavian Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allan_Octavian_Hume), a retired British [Civil Servant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Civil_Service_\(British_India\)) who was also known for his pro-Indian activities, outlined his idea for a body representing Indian interests in an open letter to graduates of the [University of Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Calcutta).[^cite_note-Gehlot_1991_35-46] The aim was to obtain a greater share in government for educated Indians and to create a platform for civic and political dialogue between them and the British Raj. Hume initiated contact with prominent leaders in India and a notice convening the first meeting of the Indian National Union to be held in [Poona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poona) the following December was issued.[^cite_note-THotINC-47] However, due to a [cholera](7591.md) outbreak in Poona it was moved to Bombay.[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-THotINC-47] Subsequently, the first session of the Indian National Congress held in Bombay from 28 to 31 December 1885 at Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College.[^cite_note-First_Session-49] Hume organised the first meeting in Bombay with the approval of the [Viceroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_India) [Lord Dufferin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Hamilton-Temple-Blackwood,_1st_Marquess_of_Dufferin_and_Ava). He assumed office as the General Secretary, while [Umesh Chandra Banerjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Womesh_Chunder_Bonnerjee) was appointed as the first president of Congress.[^cite_note-GS-50] Hume believed that while the British helped bring peace to India, they still had not solved the country’s economic problems.[^cite_note-Hume1885-51]



Leaders of the party gathered at first session of Indian National Congress, Bombay, 28–31 December 1885

The first session was attended by 72 delegates, with the majority being lawyers, representing each province of India.[^cite_note-\:1-52][^cite_note-53] Notable representatives included Scottish [ICS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Civil_Service) officer [William Wedderburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wedderburn), [Dadabhai Naoroji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dadabhai_Naoroji), [Badruddin Tyabji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badruddin_Tyabji) and [Pherozeshah Mehta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pherozeshah_Mehta) of the Bombay Presidency Association, [Ganesh Vasudeo Joshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganesh_Vasudeo_Joshi) of the [Poona Sarvajanik Sabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poona_Sarvajanik_Sabha), social reformer and newspaper editor [Gopal Ganesh Agarkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gopal_Ganesh_Agarkar), Justice [K. T. Telang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashinath_Trimbak_Telang), [N. G. Chandavarkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N._G._Chandavarkar), [Dinshaw Wacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinshaw_Wacha), [Behramji Malabari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behramji_Malabari), journalist, and activist [Gooty Kesava Pillai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gooty_Kesava_Pillai), and [P. Rangaiah Naidu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._Rangaiah_Naidu) of the [Madras Mahajana Sabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madras_Mahajana_Sabha).[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-INC-55] Notably, there were no women present at this session.[^cite_note-GS-50] During the first session, the Indian delegates presented 9 resolutions to the British authorities including; India Council in London should be abolished, creation of legislative councils for the [North-West Frontier Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-West_Frontier_Province) (NWFP), [Sindh](28972.md) and [Awadh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awadh), [Civil Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Civil_Service) Reform, and Appointment of a commission to enquire into the working of the Indian Administration from 1858- till date.[^cite_note-Resolutions1885-56] [Dadabhai Naoroji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dadabhai_Naoroji), a member of the sister [Indian National Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Association), was elected president of the Congress in 1886. He was the first Indian Member of Parliament in the [British House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_House_of_Commons) (1892–1895) and spent a large part of his life and resources campaigning for India’s cause on the international stage. In 1889, a [British branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Committee_of_the_Indian_National_Congress) of the Indian National Congress was set up in London.[^cite_note-INC_Branch-57]



[Womesh Chunder Bonnerjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Womesh_Chunder_Bonnerjee), first [president of Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_Indian_National_Congress)

In its initial years, the Congress served as a platform for politically active individuals advocating reforms within the British Empire. However, two distinct factions emerged: one group sought complete independence from British rule, while the other aimed for reforms within the existing system, with a focus on *[Indianisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianisation)*. This division marked the early phase of Congress, as different leaders and members had varied visions for the future of India, ranging from moderate reforms to a push for full sovereignty.[^cite_note-Roots-58] They primarily advocated for the 'Indianisation' of administrative services, emphasising that India should be governed by Indians, in collaboration with the British. The majority of the founding members of Congress has been educated or lived in Britain. As a result, unrepresentative of the Indian masses at the time,[^cite_note-SissonWolpert1988-59] it functioned more as a stage for elite Indian ambitions than a political party for the first two decade of its existence.[^cite_note-SissonWolpert1988_2-60]

### Early years

Since its establishment, the Congress was led by Moderate leaders, who were influenced by [Western](21208200.md) [political ideas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_philosophy), particularly [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism). They emphasised [individual dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanism), the [right to freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_freedom), and [equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_equality) for all, regardless of caste, creed, or sex. This philosophy guided them in opposing British autocracy, demanding the rule of law, equality before the law, and advocating for [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism).[^cite_note-Approach-61] However, by 1905, the party had divided into two groups, each with its own approach and ideology on how to attain [self-governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swaraj) for India. A division arose between the Moderates—led by [Dadabhai Naoroji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dadabhai_Naoroji), [Romesh Chunder Dutt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romesh_Chunder_Dutt), [Gopal Krishna Gokhale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gopal_Krishna_Gokhale), [and](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gopal_Krishna_Gokhale) [Dinshaw Wacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinshaw_Edulji_Wacha)—who believed in a peaceful and constitutional approach to achieving reforms and self-governance within the framework of the British Empire, and the Extremists, led by [Lala Lajpat Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lala_Lajpat_Rai), [Bipin Chandra Pal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipin_Chandra_Pal) and [Bal Gangadhar Tilak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bal_Gangadhar_Tilak)—who favored a more assertive and confrontational strategy.[^cite_note-Moderates-62]

The moderates preferred to avoid direct conflict with the British, aiming instead to reform their governance to better serve the country's interests. They aimed to collaborate with British authorities and use constitutional means, such as petitions, resolutions, and dialogue, to address the grievances of Indians.[^cite_note-Approach-61] Over time, as they recognised the impact of British rule, many moderate leaders shifted their stance and started advocating for *[Swaraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swaraj)* or self-government for India within the British Empire. Thereafter, the moderates followed a two-fold approach to achieve their goals. First, they aimed to build strong public opinion to inspire a sense of national consciousness and unity, while educating the masses on shared political issues. Second, they sought to influence both the [British government](25318118.md) and public opinion, advocating for reforms in India that aligned with the demands of the nationalists.[^cite_note-Approach-61] The Moderates were able to analyse the political and economic impacts of [British rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_rule) in India. Dadabhai Naoroji, Romesh Chunder Dutt, and Dinshaw Wacha and others introduced the [Drain Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dadabhai_Naoroji#Drain_theory_and_poverty) to highlight how Britain exploited India's resources.[^cite_note-Drain_Theory-63] The Drain Theory, proposed by these leaders, challenged the notion that British rule was beneficial for India, shaping a nationwide public opinion that British colonialism was the primary reason for India’s poverty and economic exploitation.[^cite_note-Poverty-64] The moderate leaders had several demands, including proper representation of Indians on the [Legislative Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Councils_Act_1892) and an increase in the powers of these councils. They also advocated for administrative reforms and voiced their opinions on international issues. They opposed the [annexation of Burma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Anglo-Burmese_War), the [military actions in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Afghan_War), and the treatment of tribal people in [northwestern India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_India). Additionally, they called for better conditions for Indian workers who had migrated to countries such as [South Africa](17416221.md), [Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Malaya), [Mauritius](19201.md), the [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies), and [British Guyana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Guyana) as [indentured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indentured_servitude) labourers.

Early Nationalists

[Bal Gangadhar Tilak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bal_Gangadhar_Tilak) speaking in 1907 as the Party split into moderates and extremists. Seated at the table is [Aurobindo Ghosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurobindo_Ghosh) and to his right (in the chair) is [G. S. Khaparde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G._S._Khaparde), both allies of Tilak.

The other faction, led by radical leaders such as [Lala Lajpat Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lala_Lajpat_Rai), [Bal Gangadhar Tilak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bal_Gangadhar_Tilak), and [Bipin Chandra Pal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipin_Chandra_Pal)—colloquially known as "Lal, Bal, Pal"—advocated a more militant approach. Known as the extremist group, or assertive nationalists, they emerged prominently after the [partition of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_Bengal_\(1905\)) in 1905. They believed in direct action and criticised the moderate approach, advocating for more aggressive means to achieve self-rule (*Swaraj*). Disillusioned by the limited achievements of the moderates, they were less willing to compromise with the British. Instead, they focused on building mass support by instilling a sense of self-respect, self-reliance, pride in their ancient heritage and national unity to attain their objectives.[^cite_note-Extremists-65] The Extremist leaders did not support the use of violence against British rule and rejected methods such as political murder and assassination. They successfully engaged the urban middle and lower classes, while also mobilising peasants and workers. Although they used religious symbols to inspire the masses, they consciously avoided mixing religion with politics. Tilak sought to mobilise Hindu Indians by appealing to an explicitly Hindu political identity displayed in the annual public [Sarvajanik Ganeshotsav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarvajanik_Ganeshotsav) and [Shiv Jayanti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiv_Jayanti) festivals that he inaugurated in western India, particularly in the [Bombay Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay_Presidency).[^cite_note-66] Tilak, along with his associates [Gopal Ganesh Agarkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gopal_Ganesh_Agarkar) and [Vishnushastri Chiplunkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vishnushastri_Chiplunkar), believed that educating the masses was the most effective way to serve the nation. In line with this belief, they co-founded the New English School in [Pune](164634.md) in 1876 to provide modern, Indian-controlled education as an alternative to British institutions.[^cite_note-NES-67] Subsequently, these leaders established the [Deccan Education Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_Education_Society) (DES) to advance their pedagogical objectives, with a particular emphasis on cultivating national consciousness among Indian youth.[^cite_note-DES-68] However, Tilak soon realised that education alone was not sufficient; he believed it was equally important to raise public awareness about the country's condition. To achieve this, he started two weekly publications in 1881: the *[Maratha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha)* in English and *[Kesari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kesari_\(Marathi_newspaper\))* in Marathi. By the end of 1905, Congress began transforming into a mass movement, largely due to the agitation against the partition of Bengal and the emergence of the [Swadeshi movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swadeshi_movement).[^cite_note-INC-55] The Moderates’ approach of gradual reforms through constitutional methods and the Extremists’ advocacy of direct action were fundamentally incompatible. These ideological disagreements between the Extremists and the Moderates caused a deep rift within the movement. At the Congress session held in Surat in December 1907, the party formally split into two factions, an event later referred to as the [Surat Split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surat_Split).[^cite_note-Surat_Split-69]

[Annie Besant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Besant), a British social reformer, moved to India in 1893 and became actively involved in the Congress.[^cite_note-Besant_India-70] Recognising the importance of full cooperation from the moderates for the success of the movement, both Tilak and Besant realised that it was necessary to secure the full cooperation of the moderates. In 1915, during the annual session of the Congress held at [Lucknow](392095.md) under the presidency of [Ambica Charan Mazumdar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambica_Charan_Mazumdar), it was decided that the extremists led by Tilak would be admitted to the Congress. Inspired by the [Irish Home Rule movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Home_Rule_movement), which sought greater autonomy from Britain, Tilak and Besant were influenced by the concept of self-government (Home Rule) and began calling for similar rights for India.[^cite_note-IHR-71] However, Tilak and Besant were unable to convince the Indian National Congress to support their proposal to set up Home Rule leagues. As a result, they established separate leagues. Tilak launched the [Indian Home Rule League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Home_Rule_League) in April 1916 at [Belgaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgaum), with its headquarters in [Poona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poona). His league operated primarily in [Maharashtra](20629.md) (excluding Bombay), [Karnataka](16880.md), and the [Central Provinces and Berar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Provinces_and_Berar).[^cite_note-IHR-71] In contrast, Besant set up her All-India Home Rule League in September 1916 in [Madras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madras), which grew to include over 200 branches across the country.[^cite_note-Besant_India-70] Prominent leaders who joined or supported the Home Rule movement included [Motilal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motilal_Nehru), [Muhammad Ali Jinnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_Jinnah), [Bhulabhai Desai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhulabhai_Desai), [Saifuddin Kitchlew, Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru), [Chittaranjan Das](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chittaranjan_Das), [Kanaiyalal Maneklal Munshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanaiyalal_Maneklal_Munshi), [Madan Mohan Malviya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madan_Mohan_Malviya), [Tej Bahadur Sapru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tej_Bahadur_Sapru), and [Lala Lajpat Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lala_Lajpat_Rai).

### Congress as a mass movement

In 1915, [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi) returned from South Africa and joined Congress.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-Sailed_1915-73] His efforts in South Africa were well known not only among the educated but also among the masses. During 1917 and 1918, Mahatma Gandhi was involved in three struggles: known as [Champaran Satyagraha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champaran_Satyagraha), Ahmedabad Mill Strike and [Kheda Satyagraha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kheda_Satyagraha_of_1918).[^cite_note-experiments-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-Mill-76] After [World War I](4764461.md), the party came to be associated with Gandhi, who remained its unofficial spiritual leader and icon.[^cite_note-M.Gandhi1-77] He formed an alliance with the [Khilafat Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khilafat_Movement) in 1920 as part of his opposition to British rule in India,[^cite_note-78] and fought for the rights for Indians using civil disobedience or *[Satyagraha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satyagraha)* as the tool for agitation.[^cite_note-79] In 1922, after the deaths of policemen at [Chauri Chaura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chauri_Chaura_incident), Gandhi suspended the agitation.



Mahatma Gandhi and Jawaharlal Nehru during a meeting of the All India Congress, in 1946

With the help of the moderate group led by Gokhale, in 1924 Gandhi became president of Congress.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-\:1-52] The rise of Gandhi's popularity and his *satyagraha* art of revolution led to support from [Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardar_Vallabhbhai_Patel), [Pandit Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandit_Jawaharlal_Nehru), [Rajendra Prasad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajendra_Prasad), [Khan Mohammad Abbas Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khan_Mohammad_Abbas_Khan), [Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khan_Abdul_Ghaffar_Khan), [Chakravarti Rajgopalachari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Rajagopalachari), [Anugrah Narayan Sinha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anugrah_Narayan_Sinha), [Jayaprakash Narayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayaprakash_Narayan), [Jivatram Kripalani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jivatram_Kripalani), and [Maulana Abul Kalam Azad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maulana_Abul_Kalam_Azad). As a result of prevailing nationalism, Gandhi's popularity, and the party's attempts at eradicating [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste) differences, [untouchability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untouchability), poverty, and religious and ethnic divisions, Congress became a forceful and dominant group.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83] Although its members were predominantly Hindu, it had members from other religions, economic classes, and ethnic and linguistic groups.[^cite_note-84]



Flag adopted by INC, 1931

At the Congress 1929 Lahore session under the presidency of Jawaharlal Nehru, *[Purna Swaraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purna_Swaraj)* (complete independence) was declared as the party's goal, declaring 26 January 1930 as *Purna Swaraj Diwas* (Independence Day).[^cite_note-Remarks-85] The same year, Srinivas Iyenger was expelled from the party for demanding full independence, not just [home rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_rule) as demanded by Gandhi.[^cite_note-86]

After the passage of the [Government of India Act 1935](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_India_Act_1935), [provincial elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1937_Indian_provincial_elections) were held in India in the winter of 1936–37 in eleven provinces: [Madras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madras_Presidency), [Central Provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Provinces), Bihar, Orissa, [United Provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Provinces_of_Agra_and_Oudh), [Bombay Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay_Presidency), Assam, NWFP, Bengal, Punjab, and Sindh. The final results of the elections were declared in February 1937.[^cite_note-Manglik2020-87] The Indian National Congress gained power in eight of them; the three exceptions being Bengal, [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_India), and Sindh.[^cite_note-Manglik2020-87] The [All-India Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-India_Muslim_League) failed to form a Government in any Province.[^cite_note-88]

Congress [Ministers](739237.md) resigned in October and November 1939 in protest against Viceroy [Lord Linlithgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Hope,_2nd_Marquess_of_Linlithgow)'s declaration that India was a belligerent in [World War II](32927.md) without consulting the Indian people.[^cite_note-89] In 1939, [Subhas Chandra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subhas_Chandra_Bose), the elected president of Congress in 1938 and 1939, resigned from Congress over the selection of the working committee.[^cite_note-90] Congress was an umbrella organisation, sheltering radical socialists, traditionalists, and Hindu and [Muslim conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conservatism). [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi) expelled all the socialist groupings, including the [Congress Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_Socialist_Party), the [Krishak Praja Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krishak_Sramik_Party), and the [Swaraj Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swaraj_Party), along with [Subhas Chandra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subhas_Chandra_Bose), in 1939.[^cite_note-M.Gandhi1-77]

After the failure of the [Cripps Mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cripps_Mission) launched by the British government to gain Indian support for the British war effort, Mahatma Gandhi made a call to "Do or Die", delivered in Bombay on 8 August 1942 at the [Gowalia Tank Maidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gowalia_Tank). Gandhi [endorsed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quit_India_speech) the [Quit India Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quit_India_Movement), opposing any help to the British cause in the [Second World War](32927.md).[^cite_note-91] The colonial government instituted mass arrests including of Gandhi and Congress leaders, and killed over 1,000 Indians who participated in this movement.[^cite_note-92] Meanwhile, a spate of violent attacks were carried out by the nationalists against the colonial government and infrastructure.[^cite_note-Anderson-93] The movement played a role in weakening British control over the South Asian region and ultimately paved the way for Indian independence.[^cite_note-Anderson-93][^cite_note-herman467-94]

In 1945, when the Second World War almost came to an end, the [Labour Party of the United Kingdom](19279158.md) won elections with a promise to provide independence to India.[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96] The jailed political prisoners of the Quit India movement were released in the same year.[^cite_note-97]

Moderate INC leaders during a meet

[Azad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abul_Kalam_Azad), [Patel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardar_Patel) and [Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi) at an AICC meeting in Bombay, 1940

In 1946, the colonial government tried the soldiers of Japanese-sponsored [Indian National Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Army) in the [INA trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INA_trials). In response, Congress helped form the [INA Defence Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INA_Defence_Committee), which assembled a legal team to defend the soldiers of the [Azad Hind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Hind) provisional government. The team included several famous lawyers, including [Bhulabhai Desai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhulabhai_Desai), [Asaf Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asaf_Ali), and Jawaharlal Nehru.[^cite_note-98] The colonial government eventually backtracked in the face of opposition by the Congress.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100]

### Post-independence

After Indian independence in 1947, the Indian National Congress became the dominant political party in the country. The long period of early dominance in the politics of the republic led to its political system sometimes being referred to as the "Congress System".[^cite_note-101] In 1952, in the [first general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Indian_general_election) held after Independence, the party swept to power in the national parliament and most state legislatures. It held power nationally until 1977 when it was defeated by the Janata coalition. It returned to power in 1980 and ruled until 1989 when it was once again defeated. The party formed the government in 1991 at the head of a coalition, as well as in 2004 and 2009 when it led the United Progressive Alliance. During this period the Congress remained centre-left in its social policies while steadily shifting from a socialist to a [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal) economic outlook.[^cite_note-102] The Party's rivals at state level have been national parties including the [Bharatiya Janata Party](149330.md) (BJP), the [Communist Party of India (Marxist)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_India_\(Marxist\)) (CPIM), and various regional parties, such as the [Telugu Desam Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telugu_Desam_Party), [Trinamool Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Trinamool_Congress) and [Aam Aadmi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aam_Aadmi_Party).[^cite_note-103]



A Congress procession including bullocks and a flag – a pair of bullocks was the election symbol of the Congress Party in 1952

A post-[partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) successor to the party survived as the [Pakistan National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_National_Congress), a party which represented the rights of religious minorities in the state. The party's support was strongest in the Bengali-speaking province of [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan). After the Bangladeshi War of Independence, it became known as the [Bangladeshi National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladeshi_National_Congress), but was dissolved in 1975 by the government.[^cite_note-A-104][^cite_note-B-105][^cite_note-C-106]

#### Nehru era (1947–1966)

See also: [Sino-Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War) and [Tashkent Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tashkent_Declaration)

From 1951 until his death in 1964, [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru) was the paramount leader of the party. Congress gained power in landslide victories in the general elections of 1951–52, 1957, and 1962.[^cite_note-career-107] During his tenure, Nehru implemented policies based on [import substitution industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Import_substitution_industrialisation), and advocated a [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) where the government-controlled [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector) co-existed with the [private sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_sector).[^cite_note-economic_policy-108] He believed the establishment of basic and heavy industries was fundamental to the development and modernisation of the Indian economy.[^cite_note-career-107] The Nehru government directed investment primarily into key public sector industries—steel, iron, coal, and power—promoting their development with subsidies and protectionist policies.[^cite_note-economic_policy-108] Nehru embraced secularism, [socialistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialistic) economic practices based on state-driven industrialisation, and a [non-aligned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) and non-confrontational foreign policy that became typical of the modern Congress Party.[^cite_note-root-109] The policy of non-alignment during the [Cold War](325329.md) meant Nehru received financial and technical support from both the [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) and [Western Blocs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc) to build India's industrial base from nothing.[^cite_note-movement-110][^cite_note-Non-Aligned-111]

See caption

[Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru) signing the [Indian Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Constitution) c.1950

During his period in office, there were four known assassination attempts on Nehru.[^cite_note-Sahgal2010-112] The first attempt on his life was during partition in 1947 while he was visiting the [North-West Frontier Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-West_Frontier_Province_\(1901%E2%80%932010\)) in a car. The second was by a knife-wielding rickshaw-puller in Maharashtra in 1955.[^cite_note-autogenerated3-113] A third attempt happened in [Bombay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay_Presidency) in 1956.[^cite_note-autogenerated2-114] The fourth was a failed bombing attempt on railway tracks in Maharashtra in 1961.[^cite_note-Sahgal2010-112] Despite threats to his life, Nehru despised having excess security personnel around him and did not like his movements to disrupt traffic.[^cite_note-Sahgal2010-112] [K. Kamaraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K._Kamaraj) became the president of the [All India Congress Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Congress_Committee) in 1963 during the last year of Nehru's life.[^cite_note-life-115] Prior to that, he had been the chief minister of [Madras state](29918.md) for nine years.[^cite_note-Kingmakers-116] Kamaraj had also been a member of "the syndicate", a group of right wing leaders within Congress. In 1963 the Congress lost popularity following the defeat in the Indo-Chinese war of 1962. To revitalise the party, Kamaraj proposed the [Kamaraj Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamaraj_Plan) to Nehru that encouraged six Congress chief ministers (including himself) and six senior cabinet ministers to resign to take up party work.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh198146-117][^cite_note-Jeyaraman2013-118][^cite_note-Gehlot1991-119]

In 1964, Nehru [died](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Jawaharlal_Nehru) because of an [aortic dissection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aortic_dissection), raising questions about the party's future.[^cite_note-death-120][^cite_note-Nehru_death-121][^cite_note-Nehru_dies-122] Following the death of Nehru, [Gulzarilal Nanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulzarilal_Nanda) was appointed as the interim prime minister on 27 May 1964, pending the election of a new parliamentary leader of the Congress party who would then become prime minister.[^cite_note-Interim_PM-123] During the leadership contest to succeed Nehru, the preference was between Morarji Desai and Lal Bahadur Shashtri. Eventually, Shashtri was selected as the next parliamentary leader thus the Prime Minister. Kamaraj was widely credited as the "kingmaker" in for ensuring the victory of [Lal Bahadur Shastri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lal_Bahadur_Shastri) over Morarji Desai.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh198142-124]



Inauguration of MNREC Building Allahabad by [Lal Bahadur Shastri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lal_Bahadur_Shastri)

As prime minister, Shastri retained most of members of Nehru's [Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_of_the_Republic_of_India); [T. T. Krishnamachari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T._T._Krishnamachari) was retained as [Finance Minister of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance_Minister_of_India), as was Defence Minister [Yashwantrao Chavan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yashwantrao_Chavan).[^cite_note-PradhanGodbole1999-125] Shastri appointed [Swaran Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swaran_Singh) to succeed him as [External Affairs Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/External_Affairs_Minister).[^cite_note-Affairs2008-126] Shastri appointed [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi), Jawaharlal Nehru's daughter and former party president, [Minister of Information and Broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Information_and_Broadcasting).[^cite_note-ministry-127] Gulzarilal Nanda continued as the [Minister of Home Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_\(India\)).[^cite_note-home_affairs-128] As [Prime Minister](24452.md), Shastri continued Nehru's policy of [non-alignment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-aligned_Movement),[^cite_note-NarayanShastri2006-129] but built closer relations with the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). In the aftermath of the [Sino-Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War) of 1962, and the formation of military ties between China and Pakistan, Shastri's government expanded the defence budget of India's armed forces. He also promoted the White Revolution—a national campaign to increase the production and supply of milk by creating the [National Dairy Development Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Dairy_Development_Board).[^cite_note-Revolution-130] The Madras anti-Hindi agitation of 1965 occurred during Shastri's tenure.[^cite_note-Dasgupta1970-131][^cite_note-conflict-132]

Shastri became a national hero following victory in the [Indo-Pakistani War of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1965).[^cite_note-war-133] His slogan, "[Jai Jawan Jai Kisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jai_Jawan_Jai_Kisan)" ("Hail the soldier, Hail the farmer"), became very popular during the war.[^cite_note-slogan-134] On 11 January 1966, a day after signing the [Tashkent Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tashkent_Declaration), Shastri died in Tashkent, reportedly of a heart attack; but the circumstances of his death remain mysterious.[^cite_note-controversy-135][^cite_note-diminutive_leader-136][^cite_note-Shashtri-137] After Shastri's death, Congress elected [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) as leader over [Morarji Desai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morarji_Desai). Once again, [K. Kamaraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K._Kamaraj) was instrumental in achieving this result. The differences among the top leadership of the Congress regarding the future of the party during resulted in the formation of several breakaway parties such as [Orissa Jana Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orissa_Jana_Congress), [Bangla Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangla_Congress), [Utkal Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utkal_Congress), and, [Bharatiya Kranti Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatiya_Kranti_Dal).

#### Indira Gandhi era (1966–1984)

Further information: [The Emergency (India)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Emergency_\(India\)), [Assassination of Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Indira_Gandhi), and [1984 anti-Sikh riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_anti-Sikh_riots)

In 1967, following a poor performance in the [1967 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Indian_general_election), [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) started moving toward the political left. On 12 July 1969, Congress Parliamentary Board nominated [Neelam Sanjiva Reddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neelam_Sanjiva_Reddy) as Congress's candidate for the post of [President of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_India) by a vote of four to two. [K. Kamaraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K._Kamaraj), [Morarji Desai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morarji_Desai) and [S. K. Patil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S._K._Patil) voted for Reddy. [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) and [Fakhruddin Ali Ahmed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fakhruddin_Ali_Ahmed) voted for [V. V. Giri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V._V._Giri) and Congress President [S. Nijalingappa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S._Nijalingappa), Home Minister [Yashwantrao Chavan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yashwantrao_Chavan) and Agriculture Minister [Jagjivan Ram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagjivan_Ram) abstained from voting.[^cite_note-\:0-138][^cite_note-139]



[Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) with U.S. President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon), 1971

In mid-1969, she was involved in a dispute with senior party leaders on several issues. Notably – Her support for the independent candidate, [V. V. Giri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V._V._Giri), rather than the official Congress party candidate, [Neelam Sanjiva Reddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neelam_Sanjiva_Reddy), for the vacant post of the [president of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_India)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh198165–80-140][^cite_note-141] and Gandhi's abrupt nationalisation of the 14 biggest banks in India.

In November 1969, the Congress party president, [S. Nijalingappa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S._Nijalingappa), expelled Indira Gandhi from the party for indiscipline.[^cite_note-socialism-142][^cite_note-expelled-143] Subsequently, Gandhi launched her own faction of the INC which came to be known as Congress (R).[^cite_note-144] The original party then came to be known as [Indian National Congress (O)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_\(O\)).[^cite_note-145] Its principal leaders were Kamraj, Morarji Desai, Nijalingappa and [S. K. Patil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S._K._Patil) who stood for a more right-wing agenda.[^cite_note-book-146] The split occurred when a united opposition under the banner of [Samyukt Vidhayak Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samyukt_Vidhayak_Dal), won control over several states in the [Hindi Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi_Belt).[^cite_note-147] Indira Gandhi, on the other side, wanted to use a populist agenda in order to mobilise popular support for the party.[^cite_note-book-146] Her faction, called Congress (R), was supported by most of the Congress MPs while [the original party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Congress_\(Organisation\)) had the support of only 65 MPs.[^cite_note-Rosser-148] In the All India Congress Committee, 446 of its 705 members walked over to Indira's side. The "Old Congress" retained the party symbol of a pair of bullocks carrying a yoke while Indira's breakaway faction was given a new symbol of a cow with a suckling calf by the Election Commission as the party election symbol. The Congress (O) eventually merged with other opposition parties to form the [Janata Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janata_Party).

> "India might be an ancient country but was a young democracy and as such should remain vigilant against the domination of few over the social, economic or political systems. Banks should be publicly owned so that they catered to not just large industries and big businesses but also agriculturists, small industries and entrepreneurs. Furthermore, the private banks had been functioning erratically with hundreds of them failing and causing loss to the depositors who were given no guarantee against such loss."

— Gandhi's remarks after the nationalisation of private banks.

[^cite_note-Quote-149]

In the mid-term [1971 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Indian_general_election), the Gandhi-led Congress (R) won a landslide victory on a platform of progressive policies such as the elimination of poverty (*[Garibi Hatao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garibi_Hatao)*).[^cite_note-1971_result-150] The policies of the Congress (R) under Gandhi before the 1971 elections included proposals to abolish the [Privy Purse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Purse_in_India) to former rulers of the [Princely states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_states), and the 1969 [nationalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalization) of India's 14 largest banks.[^cite_note-nationalisation-151] The 1969 attempt by Indira Gandhi government to abolish privy purse and the official recognition of the titles did not meet with success. The constitutional Amendment bill to this effect was passed in Lok Sabha, but it failed to get the required two-thirds majority in the Rajya Sabha. However, in 1971, with the passage of the [Twenty-sixth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-sixth_Amendment_of_the_Constitution_of_India) to the Constitution of India, the privy purses were abolished.

Due to [Sino-Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War) 1962, India faced a huge budgetary deficit resulting in its treasury being almost empty, high inflation, and dwindling forex reserves. The brief War of 1962 exposed weaknesses in the economy and shifted the focus towards the defence industry and the [Indian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Army). The government found itself short of resources to fund the Third Plan (1961–1966). [Subhadra Joshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subhadra_Joshi) a senior party member, proposed a non-official resolution asking for the nationalisation of private banks stating that nationalisation would help in mobilising resources for development.[^cite_note-Bank_Act-152] In July 1969, Indira Gandhi through the ordinance nationalised fourteen major private banks.[^cite_note-153] After being re-elected in 1971 on a campaign that endorsed nationalisation, Indira Gandhi went on to nationalise the coal, steel, copper, refining, cotton textiles and insurance industries. The main reason was to protect employment and the interest of the organised labour.[^cite_note-Bank_Act-152]

On 12 June 1975, the [High Court of Allahabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allahabad_High_Court) declared Indira Gandhi's election to the [Lok Sabha](275456.md), the lower house of India's parliament, void on the grounds of electoral malpractice.[^cite_note-emergency-154] However, Gandhi rejected calls to resign and announced plans to appeal to the [Supreme Court](395852.md). In response to increasing disorder and lawlessness, Gandhi's ministry recommended that President [Fakhruddin Ali Ahmed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fakhruddin_Ali_Ahmed) declare a [State of Emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Emergency_in_India), based on the provisions of [Article 352](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Part_Eleven_of_the_Constitution_of_India) of the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India).[^cite_note-155] During the [nineteen-month emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Emergency_\(India\)), widespread oppression and abuse of power by Gandhi's unelected younger son and political heir [Sanjay Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjay_Gandhi) and his close associates occurred.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157][^cite_note-The_Life_of_Indira_Gandhi-158] Implemented on 25 June 1975, the Emergency officially ended on 21 March 1977.[^cite_note-proved-159] All political prisoners were released and fresh elections for the Lok Sabha were called.[^cite_note-union-160] In [parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Indian_general_election) held in March, the Janata alliance of anti-Indira opposition parties won a landslide victory over Congress, winning 295 seats in the Lok Sabha against Congress' 153. Gandhi lost her seat to her Janata opponent [Raj Narain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raj_Narain).

On 2 January 1978, Indira and her followers seceded and formed a new opposition party, popularly called Congress (I)—the "I" signifying Indira.[^cite_note-Buffalo_state-161][^cite_note-IE-162][^cite_note-163] During the next year, her new party attracted enough members of the legislature to become the official opposition.[^cite_note-Basu2016-164] In November 1978, Gandhi regained a parliamentary seat. In January 1980, following a [landslide victory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Indian_general_election) for Congress (I), she was again elected prime minister.[^cite_note-1980_report-165] The national election commission declared Congress (I) to be the real Indian National Congress for the [1984 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Indian_general_election).[^cite_note-IE_1984-166] However, the designation I was dropped only in 1996.[^cite_note-Basu2016-164][^cite_note-1980_report-165]

Gandhi's premiership witnessed increasing turmoil in [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_India), with demands for [Sikh autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalistan) by [Jarnail Singh Bhindranwale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarnail_Singh_Bhindranwale) and his militant followers.[^cite_note-operation-167] In 1983, Bhindranwale with his armed followers headquartered themselves in the [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmandir_Sahib) in [Amritsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amritsar) and started accumulating weapons.[^cite_note-telegraph_report-168] In June 1984, after several futile negotiations, Gandhi ordered the [Indian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Army) to enter the Golden Temple to establish control over the complex and remove Bhindranwale and his armed followers. This event is known as [Operation Blue Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Blue_Star).[^cite_note-blue_star-169] On 31 October 1984, two of Gandhi's bodyguards, [Satwant Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satwant_Singh) and [Beant Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beant_Singh_\(assassin\)), [shot her](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Indira_Gandhi) with their service weapons in the garden of the prime minister's residence in response to her authorisation of Operation Blue Star.[^cite_note-telegraph_report-168] Gandhi was due to be interviewed by British actor [Peter Ustinov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Ustinov), who was filming a documentary for Irish television.[^cite_note-assassination-170] Her assassination prompted the [1984 anti-Sikh riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_anti-Sikh_riots), during which 3,000–17,000 people were killed.[^cite_note-violence-171][^cite_note-SAGE-172][^cite_note-telegraph-173][^cite_note-174] Congress Party MP, [Sajjan Kumar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajjan_Kumar), was convicted in two cases for instigating and leading a mob to attack and murder Sikh civilians during the Anti-Sikh riots in New Delhi.[^cite_note-toi-Sajjan-conviction-175]

#### Rajiv and Sonia Gandhi era (1984–2014)

See also: [Indian intervention in the Sri Lankan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_intervention_in_the_Sri_Lankan_Civil_War) and [Economic liberalisation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India)

In 1984, Indira Gandhi's son [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi) became nominal head of Congress, and went on to become prime minister upon her assassination.[^cite_note-pm_rajiv-176] In December, he led Congress to a landslide victory, where it secured 401 seats in the parliament.[^cite_note-1984_election_result-177] His administration took measures to reform the government bureaucracy and liberalise the country's economy.[^cite_note-tenure-178] Rajiv Gandhi's attempts to discourage separatist movements in Punjab and Kashmir backfired. After his government became embroiled in several financial scandals, his leadership became increasingly ineffectual.[^cite_note-movements-179] Gandhi was regarded as a non-abrasive person who consulted other party members and refrained from hasty decisions.[^cite_note-consult-180] The [Bofors scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bofors_scandal) damaged his reputation as an honest politician, but he was posthumously cleared of bribery allegations in 2004.[^cite_note-181] On 21 May 1991, Gandhi was killed by a bomb concealed in a basket of flowers carried by a woman associated with the [Tamil Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_Tigers_of_Tamil_Eelam).[^cite_note-autogenerated1-182] He was campaigning in Tamil Nadu for upcoming [parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Indian_general_election_in_Tamil_Nadu). In 1998, an Indian court convicted 26 people in the conspiracy to assassinate Gandhi.[^cite_note-kiled-183] The conspirators, who consisted of Tamil militants from Sri Lanka and their Indian allies, had sought revenge against Gandhi because the Indian troops he sent to Sri Lanka in 1987 to help enforce [a peace accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Sri_Lanka_Accord) there had fought with Tamil Militant guerrillas.[^cite_note-RajuRaju2008-184][^cite_note-Murder-185]

refer caption

[Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi), [Prime Minister of India](24452.md) (1984–1989) addressing the Special Session of the United Nations on [Disarmament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Office_for_Disarmament_Affairs), in New York City in June 1988

The mid-1990s marked a period of political flux in India, with frequent changes in government and coalition dynamics. Rajiv Gandhi was succeeded as party leader by [P. V. Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao), who was elected prime minister in June 1991.[^cite_note-rao-186]

His rise to the prime ministership was politically significant because he was the first person from South India to hold the office, marking a shift from the traditionally northern-dominated leadership in Indian politics. After the election, he formed a minority government. Rao himself did not contest elections in 1991, but after he was sworn in as prime minister, he won in a by-election from [Nandyal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nandyal_\(Lok_Sabha_constituency\)) in Andhra Pradesh.[^cite_note-Rao_AP_Won-187] His administration oversaw major [economic change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India) and experienced several domestic incidents that affected India's national security.[^cite_note-Reforming-188] Rao, who held the [Industries portfolio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Commerce_and_Industry_\(India\)), was personally responsible for the dismantling of the [Licence Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licence_Raj), which came under the purview of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry.[^cite_note-DNAArticle-189] Rao accelerated the dismantling of the Licence Raj, reversing the socialist policies of previous governments.[^cite_note-reinvention-190][^cite_note-policies_by_rao-191] He employed Manmohan Singh as his finance minister to begin historic economic changes. With Rao's mandate, Singh launched reforms for India's [globalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalisation) that involved implementing [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) (IMF) policies to prevent India's impending [economic collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_India_economic_crisis).[^cite_note-DNAArticle-189] Future prime ministers [Atal Bihari Vajpayee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atal_Bihari_Vajpayee) and Manmohan Singh continued the economic reform policies begun by Rao's government. He is often called the "Father of Indian economic reforms".[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-Narasimha_Rao_was_father_of_economic_reform:_Pranab-193] Rao was also referred to as *[Chanakya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanakya)* for his ability to push tough economic and political legislation through the parliament while heading a minority government.[^cite_note-frontline-194][^cite_note-195]

refer caption

Visit of erstwhile Minister for Foreign Affairs, [P. V. Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao) to [Gaston Thorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaston_Thorn), the then [President of the European Commission](24468.md)

By 1996, the party found itself in a complex political landscape. It faced internal challenges, including factionalism and leadership struggles, allegations of corruption, and a degree of anti-incumbency sentiment. The [1996 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_general_elections) witnessed the emergence of a fractured mandate, leading to the absence of a clear majority for any single party. Congress was reduced to 140 seats in elections that year, its lowest number in the Lok Sabha yet. Rao later resigned as prime minister and, in September, as party president.[^cite_note-resign-196] He was succeeded as president by [Sitaram Kesri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitaram_Kesri), the party's first non-[Brahmin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmin) leader.[^cite_note-Kesari-197] During the tenure of both Rao and Kesri, the two leaders conducted internal elections to the Congress working committees and their own posts as party presidents.[^cite_note-MitraEnskat2004-198]

The [1998 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Indian_general_election) saw Congress win 141 seats in the Lok Sabha, its lowest tally until then.[^cite_note-199] To boost its popularity and improve its performance in the forthcoming election, Congress leaders urged [Sonia Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Gandhi), Rajiv Gandhi's widow, to assume leadership of the party.[^cite_note-200] She had previously declined offers to become actively involved in party affairs and had stayed away from politics.[^cite_note-201] After her election as party leader, a section of the party that objected to the choice because of her Italian ethnicity broke away and formed the [Nationalist Congress Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist_Congress_Party) (NCP), led by [Sharad Pawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharad_Pawar).[^cite_note-202]

Sonia Gandhi struggled to revive the party in her early years as its president; she was under continuous scrutiny for her foreign birth and lack of political acumen. In the snap elections called by the [National Democratic Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance_\(India\)) (NDA) government in [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Indian_general_election), Congress' tally further plummeted to just 114 seats.[^cite_note-negi-203] Although the leadership structure was unaltered as the party campaigned strongly in the assembly elections that followed, Gandhi began to make such strategic changes as abandoning the party's 1998 Pachmarhi resolution of *ekla chalo* (go it alone) policy, and formed alliances with other like-minded parties. In the intervening years, the party was successful at various legislative assembly elections; at one point, Congress ruled 15 states.[^cite_note-204] For the [2004 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indian_general_election), Congress forged alliances with regional parties including the NCP and the [Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravida_Munnetra_Kazhagam).[^cite_note-205] The party's campaign emphasised social inclusion and the welfare of the common masses—an ideology that Gandhi herself endorsed for Congress during her presidency—with slogans such as *Congress ka haath, aam aadmi ke saath* ("Congress hand in hand with the common man"), contrasting with the NDA's "[India Shining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_Shining)" campaign.[^cite_note-negi-203][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] The Congress-led United Progressive Alliance (UPA) won 222 seats in the new parliament, defeating the NDA by a substantial margin. With the subsequent support of the communist front, Congress won a majority and formed a new government.[^cite_note-Naqshbandi-208]



11th [President of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_India) [A. P. J. Abdul Kalam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._P._J._Abdul_Kalam) authorising the Prime Minister designate [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) to form the next Government in New Delhi on 19 May 2004

Despite massive support from within the party, Gandhi declined the post of prime minister, choosing to appoint [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) instead.[^cite_note-209] She remained as party president and headed the [National Advisory Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Advisory_Council) (NAC).[^cite_note-210] During its first term in office, the UPA government passed several social reform bills. These included an [employment guarantee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi_National_Rural_Employment_Guarantee_Act) bill, the [Right to Information Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_Information_Act), and a [right to education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_Children_to_Free_and_Compulsory_Education_Act) act. The NAC, as well as [the Left Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left_Front_\(West_Bengal\)) that supported the government from the outside, were widely seen as being the driving force behind such legislation. The Left Front withdrew its support of the government over disagreements about the [U.S.–India Civil Nuclear Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93India_Civil_Nuclear_Agreement). Despite the effective loss of 62 seats in parliament, the government survived the trust vote that followed.[^cite_note-211]

In the [Lok Sabha elections held soon after](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Indian_general_election), Congress won 207 seats, the highest tally of any party since 1991. The UPA won 262, enabling it to form a government for the second time. The social welfare policies of the first UPA government, and the perceived divisiveness of the BJP, are broadly credited with the victory.[^cite_note-212]

#### Rahul Gandhi era (2014–present)

By the [2014 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indian_general_election), the party had lost much of its popular support, mainly growing discontent over a series of corruption allegations involving government officials, including the [2G spectrum case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2G_spectrum_case) and the [Indian coal allocation scam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_coal_allocation_scam), as well as the ineptness towards national security, particularly the insensitivity in the aftermath of the [2011 Mumbai bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Mumbai_bombings).[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-2G-214][^cite_note-coal-215] The Congress won only 44 seats in the [Lok Sabha](275456.md), compared to the 336 of the BJP and the NDA.[^cite_note-performance-216] The UPA suffered a landslide defeat, which was the party's worst-ever national electoral performance with its vote share dipping below 20 per cent for the first time.[^cite_note-result-217] Sonia Gandhi retired as party president in December 2017, having served for a record nineteen years. She was succeeded by her son [Rahul Gandhi](305010.md), who was elected unopposed in the 2017 INC presidential election.[^cite_note-Naqshbandi-208]

Gandhi during Bharat Jodo Yatra

Former INC president [Rahul Gandhi](305010.md) during [Bharat Jodo Yatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharat_Jodo_Yatra)

Rahul Gandhi resigned from his post after the [2019 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indian_general_election), due to the party's dismal electoral performance.[^cite_note-218] The party only won 52 seats, eight more than the previous election. Its vote percentage once again fell below 20 per cent. Following Gandhi's resignation, party leaders began deliberations for a suitable candidate to replace him. The Congress Working Committee met on 10 August to make a final decision on the matter and passed a resolution asking Sonia Gandhi to take over as interim president until a consensus candidate could be picked.[^cite_note-2019_president-219][^cite_note-220] Following the election, [Adhir Ranjan Chowdhury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adhir_Ranjan_Chowdhury) was chosen as the leader of the Congress in the Lok Sabha,[^cite_note-Pioneer-221] [Gaurav Gogoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaurav_Gogoi) was chosen as the deputy leader in Lok Sabha, and [Ravneet Singh Bittu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravneet_Singh_Bittu) was chosen as the party whip.[^cite_note-222] Based on an analysis of the candidates' poll affidavits, a report by the National Election Watch (NEW) and the [Association for Democratic Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_Democratic_Reforms) (ADR) says that, the Congress has highest [political defection](383663.md) rate since 2014. According to the report, a total of 222 electoral candidates had left the Congress to join other parties during elections held between 2014 and 2021, as 177 MPs and MLAs quit the party.[^cite_note-Analysis-223] The defections resulted in a loss of the party's established governments in [Arunachal Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015%E2%80%932016_Arunachal_Pradesh_political_crisis), [Madhya Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Madhya_Pradesh_political_crisis), [Goa](40010153.md), [Karnataka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Karnataka_political_crisis), [Puducherry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puducherry_\(union_territory\)), and [Manipur](422642.md).

On 28 August 2022, the [Congress Working Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_Working_Committee) (CWC) held an [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Indian_National_Congress_presidential_election) for the next president of the INC, to succeed Rahul Gandhi. The election was held on 17 October 2022 and counting took place on 19 October 2022.[^cite_note-224] The candidates in the race were Kerala MP [Shashi Tharoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shashi_Tharoor) and Karnataka MP [Mallikarjun Kharge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mallikarjun_Kharge).[^cite_note-225] Mallikarjun Kharge won the election in a landslide,[^cite_note-indiatimes1-1] securing 7,897 out of the 9,385 votes cast. His rival, Shashi Tharoor, secured 1,072 votes.[^cite_note-thehindu1-2]

During Kharge's presidency, the Congress party launched the [Bharat Jodo Yatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharat_Jodo_Yatra) in September 2022 as a nationwide mass outreach campaign led by [Rahul Gandhi](305010.md).[^cite_note-BJY-226] The march began in [Kanyakumari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanyakumari), [Tamil Nadu](29918.md), and concluded in [Srinagar](294148.md), [Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md), covering approximately 4,000 km (2,500 mi) on foot across multiple states.[^cite_note-Locations-227] According to the party, the yatra aimed to promote national unity and highlight issues including unemployment, inflation, and social polarization. The campaign received extensive media coverage and became a major political initiative of the party.[^cite_note-BJY-226] In January 2024, Kharge inaugurated the [Bharat Jodo Nyay Yatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharat_Jodo_Nyay_Yatra), again led by Rahul Gandhi.[^cite_note-BJY-226] Beginning in [Manipur](422642.md) and concluding in [Mumbai](19189.md), the yatra focused on the theme of "Nyay" (justice), with emphasis on economic, social, and political issues such as employment, social justice, and democratic institutions.[^cite_note-BJNY-228] The campaign formed part of the Congress party's outreach efforts ahead of the 2024 Indian general election.[^cite_note-BJNY-228]

refer caption

[Mallikarjun Kharge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mallikarjun_Kharge), INC's incumbent president and [LoP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_in_Rajya_Sabha) in [Rajya Sabha](232210.md)

Kharge led the party into the [2024 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indian_general_election), in which the party nearly doubled its Lok Sabha representation from 52 to 99 seats.[^cite_note-doubled-229] The party made significant gains in [Uttar Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indian_general_election_in_Uttar_Pradesh), [Maharashtra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indian_general_election_in_Maharashtra), [Rajasthan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indian_general_election_in_Rajasthan), [Haryana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indian_general_election_in_Haryana), and several other states, while improving its national vote share to about 21.2 per cent.[^cite_note-US_Gov-230] Although it remained well below its historical electoral peaks, the result was its best performance since 2014 and enabled Rahul Gandhi to become the [Leader of the Opposition in the Lok Sabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_in_the_Lok_Sabha), as the party crossed the 10 per cent threshold (55 seats) required for official recognition.[^cite_note-IE_LoP-231] The Congress also played a central role within the [Indian National Developmental Inclusive Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Developmental_Inclusive_Alliance) (INDIA), a coalition formed in 2023 that won 234 seats and prevented the Bharatiya Janata Party from securing a parliamentary majority on its own, forcing it to rely on its National Democratic Alliance partners to form the government.[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233]

## General election results

See also: [Electoral history of the Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_history_of_the_Indian_National_Congress)

In the [first parliamentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951%E2%80%9352_Indian_general_election) elections held in 1952, the INC won 364 seats, which was 76 per cent of the 479 contested seats.[^cite_note-India_Today_2007-234] The vote share of the INC was 45 per cent of all votes cast.[^cite_note-Ganguly_2022-235] Till the [1971 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Indian_general_election), the party's voting percentage remained intact at 40 per cent. However, the [1977 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Indian_general_election) resulted in a heavy defeat for the INC. Many notable INC party leader lost their seats, winning only 154 seats in the Lok Sabha.[^cite_note-Analysisb-236] The INC again returned to power in the [1980 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Indian_general_election) securing a 42.7 per cent vote share of all votes, winning 353 seats. INC's vote share kept increasing till 1980 and then to a record high of 48.1 per cent by 1984/85. [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi) on assuming the post of prime minister in October 1984 recommended [early elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Indian_general_election). The general elections were to be held in January 1985; instead, they were held in December 1984. The Congress won an overwhelming majority, securing 415 seats out of 533, the largest ever majority in independent India's Lok Sabha elections history.[^cite_note-Hindustan_Times_2003-237] This winning recorded a vote share of 49.1 per cent resulting in an overall increase to 48.1 per cent. The party got 32.14 per cent of voters in polls held in [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_India) and [Assam](186162.md) in 1985.[^cite_note-Ganguly_2022-235]

In November 1989, general elections were held to elect the members of the 9th Lok Sabha.[^cite_note-Anon-238] The Congress did badly in the elections, though it still managed to be the largest single party in the Lok Sabha. Its vote share started decreasing to 39.5 per cent in the 1989 general elections. The 13th Lok Sabha term was to end in October 2004, but the [National Democratic Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance) (NDA) government decided on early polls. The Lok Sabha was dissolved in February itself and the country went to the polls in April–May 2004. The INC, led by Sonia Gandhi unexpectedly emerged as the single largest party. After the elections, Congress joined up with minor parties to form the [United Progressive Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance) (UPA). The UPA with external support from the [Bahujan Samaj Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahujan_Samaj_Party), [Samajwadi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samajwadi_Party), Kerala Congress, and the Left Front managed a comfortable majority.[^cite_note-2004_Result-239] Congress has lost nearly 20% of its vote share in general elections held between 1996 and 2009.[^cite_note-Analysis-223]
