### Social affairs

The Congress party emphasises [social equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_equality), [freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_\(political\)), [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism), and [equal opportunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_opportunity).[^cite_note-NSGehlot1991-264] Its political position is generally considered to be in the centre.[^cite_note-British-Journal-42] Historically, the party has represented farmers, labourers, and [Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rural_Employment_Guarantee_Act,_2005) (MGNREGA).[^cite_note-MGNREGA-265] The MGNREGA was initiated with the objective of "enhancing livelihood security in rural areas by providing at least 100 days of guaranteed wage employment in a financial year, to every household whose adult members volunteer to do unskilled manual work." Another aim of MGNREGA is to create durable assets (such as roads, canals, ponds, and wells).[^cite_note-MGNREGA-265]

The Congress has positioned itself as both pro-Hindu and protector of the minorities. The party supports [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi)'s doctrine of *[Sarva Dharma Sama Bhava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarva_Dharma_Sama_Bhava)*, collectively termed by its party members as secularism. On 9 November 1989, Rajiv Gandhi had allowed *Shilanyas* (foundation stone-laying ceremony) adjacent to the then disputed [Ram Janmabhoomi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Janmabhoomi) site.[^cite_note-Ram_Temple_Rajiv-266] Subsequently, his government faced heavy criticism over the passing of [The Muslim Women (Protection of Rights on Divorce) Act 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Muslim_Women_\(Protection_of_Rights_on_Divorce\)_Act_1986), which nullified the Supreme Court's judgment in the [Shah Bano case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Bano_case). The [SC/ST Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheduled_Caste_and_Scheduled_Tribe_\(Prevention_of_Atrocities\)_Act,_1989) was also enacted in 1989 during the Rajiv Gandhi-led Congress government, with the objective of preventing atrocities against members of the [Scheduled Castes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheduled_Castes) and [Scheduled Tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheduled_Tribes) and providing special legal safeguards for affected communities. The [1984 violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_anti-Sikh_riots) damaged the Congress's party moral argument over secularism. The BJP questioned the Congress party's moral authority in questioning it about the [2002 Gujarat riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Gujarat_riots).[^cite_note-ThePrint-267] The Congress has distanced itself from [Hindutva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindutva) ideology, though the party has softened its stance after defeat in the 2014 and 2019 general elections.[^cite_note-Hindutva-268]

Under Narsimha Rao's premiership, the [Panchayati Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panchayati_raj_in_India) and [Municipal Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_corporation_\(India\)) got constitutional status. With the enactment of the 73rd and 74th amendments to the constitution, a new chapter, Part- IX (Panchayats) and Part IX-A (Municipalities) added to the constitution.[^cite_note-73rd-269] States have been given the flexibility to take into consideration their geographical, politico-administrative, and other consideration while adopting the Panchayati-raj system. In both panchayats and municipal bodies, in an attempt to ensure that there is inclusiveness in [local self-government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_India), reservations for SC/ST and women were implemented.[^cite_note-Self_Governance-270]

After independence, Congress advocated the idea of establishing [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi) as the sole [national language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_language) of India. The [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India), in Part XVII and specifically Article 351, addresses the promotion and development of Hindi. This article instructs the [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Government) to promote the spread of Hindi so that it can serve as a medium to express India’s diverse culture and be enriched by incorporating elements from other languages without losing its essential character. Nehru led the faction of the Congress party which promoted Hindi as the *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)* of the Indian nation.[^cite_note-Lingua-271] However, the non-Hindi-speaking Indian states, especially [Tamil Nadu](29918.md), opposed it and wanted the continued use of the English language. Lal Bahadur Shastri's tenure witnessed several protests and riots including the Madras [anti-Hindi agitation of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Hindi_agitations_of_Tamil_Nadu).[^cite_note-Tamil_protest-272] Shashtri's appealed to agitators to withdraw the movement and assured them that the English would continue to be used as the official language as long as the non-Hindi speaking states wanted.[^cite_note-Assurance-273] Indira Gandhi assuaged the sentiments of the non-Hindi speaking states by getting the Official Languages Act amended in 1967 to provide that the use of English could continue until a resolution to end the use of the language was passed by the legislature of every state that had not adopted use Hindi as its official language, and by each house of the Indian Parliament.[^cite_note-Language_1967-274] This was a guarantee of de facto use of both Hindi and English as official languages, thus establishing bilingualism in India.[^cite_note-Language_Act-275] The step led to the end of the anti-Hindi protests and riots in states.

Following the [Supreme Court](395852.md)’s final 2018 decision to abolish [Section 377](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_377_of_the_Indian_Penal_Code), the INC reiterated that personal freedom must be upheld for all citizens. Section 377 of the Indian Penal Code, which, among other things, criminalises homosexuality; former Congress president Rahul Gandhi said, "Sexuality is a matter of personal freedom and should be left to individuals". Leading party figure and former Finance Minister [P. Chidambaram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._Chidambaram) stated that the *[Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navtej_Singh_Johar_v._Union_of_India)* judgment must be quickly reversed". On 18 December 2015, [Shashi Tharoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shashi_Tharoor) leading member of the party introduced a [private member's bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_member's_bill) to replace Section 377 in the Indian Penal Code and decriminalise consensual same-sex relations. The bill was defeated in the first reading. In March 2016, Tharoor again reintroduce the private member's bill to decriminalise homosexuality but was voted down for the second time.[^cite_note-Tharoor377-276]

### Economic policies

See also: [Economic liberalisation in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India)

The economic policy of Congress-led governments can broadly be divided into two phases. The first phase, spanning from independence in 1947 to 1991, placed significant emphasis on the public sector as a driver of growth.[^cite_note-277] The second phase began with the [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization) reforms of 1991, marking a shift towards market-oriented policies. At present, the Congress Party supports a mixed economy model, wherein both the state and the private sector play active roles—combining elements of [market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) and [planned economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy). The party advocates for import substitution industrialisation, encouraging domestic production as a replacement for imports, while also supporting continued liberalisation to accelerate economic development.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] It promotes a [progressive tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_tax) structure aimed at expanding public services and addressing economic inequality by ensuring that higher-income individuals contribute a fairer share in taxes.[^cite_note-Taxation-280][^cite_note-Billionaire_tax-281]

refer caption

&#x20;Then-Finance Minister [Pranab Mukherjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pranab_Mukherjee) during the [World Economic Summit](172185.md) 2009 in New Delhi

At the beginning of the first period, erstwhile prime minister Jawaharlal Nehru implemented policies based on [import substitution industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Import_substitution_industrialization) and advocated a [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) where the government-controlled [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector) would co-exist with the [private sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_sector). He believed that the development and modernisation of India’s economy required the establishment of basic and heavy industries. The government, therefore, directed investment primarily into key public-sector industries—steel, iron, coal, and power—promoting their development with subsidies and protectionist policies. This period was called the [Licence Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licence_Raj), or Permit Raj,[^cite_note-282] which was the elaborate system of [licences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_licensing_in_India), regulations, and accompanying [red tape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_tape) that were required to set up and run businesses in India between 1947 and 1990.[^cite_note-283] The Licence Raj was a result of Nehru and his successors' desire to have a [planned economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy) where all aspects of the economy were controlled by the state, and licences were given to a select few. Private companies were required to obtain numerous approvals from various government departments before they could begin production, and even after receiving licences, their operations were closely regulated by the state. The government also prevented firms from laying off workers or closing factories.[^cite_note-BBC_UK-284] The Licence Raj system continued under Indira Gandhi, during whose tenure key sectors such as banking, steel, coal, and oil were nationalised.[^cite_note-Rosser-148][^cite_note-Kapila1-285] Under the premiership of Rajiv Gandhi, the first steps toward economic liberalisation were initiated.[^cite_note-princeton.edu-286] These included a reduction in import duties, the introduction of export incentives, and the implementation of initial tax reforms. Restrictions on company assets were also relaxed during his administration.[^cite_note-princeton.edu-286][^cite_note-287]

In 1991, the new Congress government, led by [P. V. Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao), initiated reforms to avert the impending [1991 economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_India_economic_crisis).[^cite_note-Narasimha_Rao_was_father_of_economic_reform:_Pranab-193][^cite_note-Ghosh-288] The reforms known as [New Economic Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Economic_Policy) (NEP) or "1991 economic reforms" or "LPG reforms", progressed furthest in opening up areas to [foreign investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment), reforming [capital markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_markets), [deregulating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation) domestic business, and reforming the trade regime. The reforms were implemented during a time when India grappled with a balance of payments crisis, elevated inflation, underperforming public sector undertakings (PSUs), and a substantial fiscal deficit.[^cite_note-LPG_Rao-289] It also aimed to transition the economy from a socialist model to a market economy.[^cite_note-Economy_Reforms-290] The goals of Rao's government were to reduce the [fiscal deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_deficit), [privatise](24661.md) the public sector, and increase investment in infrastructure.[^cite_note-291] Trade reforms and changes in the regulation of [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) were introduced to open India to foreign trade while stabilising external loans.[^cite_note-292] Rao chose [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) for the job. Singh, an acclaimed economist and former [governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_the_Reserve_Bank_of_India) of the [Reserve Bank of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_India), played a central role in implementing these reforms.[^cite_note-293]

In 2004, Singh became prime minister of the Congress-led UPA government. Singh remained prime minister after the UPA won the 2009 general elections. The UPA government introduced policies aimed at reforming the banking and financial sectors, as well as public sector companies.[^cite_note-ISAS-294] It also introduced policies aimed at relieving farmers of their debt.[^cite_note-295] In 2005, [Singh government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Manmohan_Singh_ministry) introduced the [value-added tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_added_tax), replacing the [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax). India was able to resist the worst effects of the global [economic crisis of 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_crisis_of_2008).[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297] Singh's government continued the [Golden Quadrilateral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Quadrilateral), the Indian highway modernisation program that was initiated by [Vajpayee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atal_Bihari_Vajpayee)'s government.[^cite_note-298] Then Finance Minister of India [Pranab Mukherjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pranab_Mukherjee) implemented many tax reforms, notably scrapping the [Fringe Benefits Tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fringe_Benefits_Tax_\(India\)) and the Commodities Transaction Tax.[^cite_note-299] He implemented the [Goods and Services Tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_\(India\)) (GST) during his tenure.[^cite_note-300] His reforms were well received by major corporate executives and economists. The introduction of retrospective taxation, however, has been criticised by some economists.[^cite_note-301] Mukherjee expanded funding for several social sector schemes including the [Jawaharlal Nehru National Urban Renewal Mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru_National_Urban_Renewal_Mission) (JNNURM). He also supported budget increases for improving [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_in_India) and health care. He expanded infrastructure programmes such as the [National Highway Development Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Highway_Development_Programme).[^cite_note-302] Electricity coverage was also expanded during his tenure. Mukherjee also reaffirmed his commitment to the principle of fiscal prudence as some economists expressed concern about the rising fiscal deficits during his tenure, the highest since 1991. Mukherjee declared the expansion in government spending was only temporary.[^cite_note-303]

### National defence and home affairs



[Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) and his wife during the passing out parade at the Platinum Jubilee Course of IMA on 10 December 2007; with foreign gentleman cadets

Since its independence, India was in pursuing of nuclear capabilities, as Nehru felt that nuclear energy could take the country forward and help achieve its developmental goals.[^cite_note-Vision-304] Consequently, Nehru began to seek assistance from the United Kingdom, Canada and the United States.[^cite_note-Skardon2010-305][^cite_note-Assistance-306] In 1958 the government of India with the help of [Homi J. Bhabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homi_J._Bhabha) adopted a three-phase power production plan and the [Nuclear Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhabha_Atomic_Research_Centre) was established in 1954.[^cite_note-Perkovich2001-307] Indira Gandhi witnessed continuous nuclear testing by [China](5405.md) from 1964 onwards, which she considered an existential threat to India.[^cite_note-JSTOR-308][^cite_note-LATimes1998-309] India conducted its first nuclear test in the [Pokhran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pokhran) desert in Rajasthan on 18 May 1974, under the name [Operation Smiling Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Smiling_Buddha).[^cite_note-First_Test-310] India asserted that the test was for "[peaceful purposes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_nuclear_explosion)", However, the test was criticised by other countries and the United States and Canada suspended all nuclear support to India.[^cite_note-PNE-311] Despite intense international criticism, the nuclear test was domestically popular and caused an immediate revival of Indira Gandhi's popularity, which had flagged considerably from its heights after the [1971 war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971).[^cite_note-Buddha-312][^cite_note-Popularity-313]

On internal security and home affairs, the Congress party promotes a federal approach to governance, emphasising cooperative federalism. The transition to statehood for parts of [Northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India) was successfully overseen under Indira Gandhi's premiership.[^cite_note-Statehood_NE-314] In 1972, her administration granted statehood to [Meghalaya](326019.md), Manipur and [Tripura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripura), while the [North-East Frontier Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-East_Frontier_Agency) was declared a union territory and renamed [Arunachal Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arunachal_Pradesh).[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316] This was followed by the [annexation of Sikkim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_Sikkimese_monarchy_referendum) in 1975.[^cite_note-317] In the late 1960s and 1970s, Gandhi ordered the Indian army to [militant Communist uprisings](4760079.md#Violence_in_West_Bengal) in the state of West Bengal. The [Naxalite–Maoist insurgency](21636599.md) in India was entirely suppressed during the [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Emergency_\(India\)).[^cite_note-Naxalite-318] Rajiv Gandhi played a pivotal role in facilitating the [Mizoram Peace Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizoram_Peace_Accord) of 1986 by personally supporting negotiations with [MNF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizo_National_Front) leader [Laldenga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laldenga) and advocating a peaceful, political solution.[^cite_note-MNF_Research-319] His premiership ensured key concessions, including full statehood for Mizoram and the integration of former insurgents into democratic politics, leading to one of India's most successful peace settlements.[^cite_note-Lessons-320]

Manmohan Singh's administration initiated a massive reconstruction effort in Kashmir to stabilise the region and strengthened anti-terrorism laws with amendments to the [Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unlawful_Activities_\(Prevention\)_Act) (UAPA).[^cite_note-UAPA-321] After a period of initial success, insurgent infiltration and terrorism in Kashmir have escalated since 2009. However, the Singh administration was successful in reducing terrorism in Northeast India.[^cite_note-Buzz7-322] Under the background of the [Punjab insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab_insurgency), the [Terrorist and Disruptive Activities (Prevention) Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorist_and_Disruptive_Activities_\(Prevention\)_Act) (TADA) was passed. The aim of the law is mainly directed toward eliminating the infiltrators from Pakistan. The law gave wide powers to [law enforcement agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_agencies) for dealing with national terrorist and socially disruptive activities. The police were not obliged to produce a detainee before a judicial magistrate within 24 hours. The law was widely criticised by human rights organisations. After the [November 2008 Mumbai terror attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Mumbai_attacks), the UPA government created the [National Investigation Agency](20712530.md) (NIA), in response to the need for a central agency to combat terrorism.[^cite_note-323] The Unique Identification Authority of India was established in February 2009 to implement the proposed [Multipurpose National Identity Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multipurpose_National_Identity_Card), to increase national security.[^cite_note-324]

### Education and healthcare

The Congress government under Nehru oversaw the establishment of many institutions of higher learning, including the [All India Institute of Medical Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Institute_of_Medical_Sciences), the [Indian Institutes of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Institutes_of_Technology), the [Indian Institutes of Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Institutes_of_Management) and the [National Institutes of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Technology). The [National Council of Educational Research and Training](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_of_Educational_Research_and_Training) (NCERT) was established in 1961 as a literary, scientific, and charitable Society under the Societies Registration Act.[^cite_note-NCERT-325] Jawahar Lal Nehru outlined a commitment in his [five-year plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-Year_Plans_of_India) to guarantee free and compulsory primary education to all of India's children. Rajiv Gandhi's premiership pioneered public information infrastructure and innovation in India.[^cite_note-Rajiv_Modern-326] His government allowed the import of fully assembled [motherboards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motherboards), which led to the price of computers being reduced.[^cite_note-computer-327] The concept of having [Navodaya Vidyalaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navodaya_Vidyalaya) in every district of India was born as a part of the [National Policy on Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Policy_on_Education) (NPE).[^cite_note-NPE_TOI-328]

In 2005, The Congress-led government started the National Rural Health Mission, which employed about 500,000 community health workers. It was praised by economist [Jeffrey Sachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Sachs).[^cite_note-timepoverty-329] In 2006, it implemented a proposal to reserve 27 per cent of seats in the All India Institute of Medical Studies (AIIMS), the [Indian Institutes of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Institutes_of_Technology) (IITs), the [Indian Institutes of Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Institutes_of_Management) (IIMs), and other central higher education institutions, for [Other Backward Classes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Other_Backward_Classes), which led to the [2006 Indian anti-reservation protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Indian_anti-reservation_protests).[^cite_note-330] The Singh government also continued the [Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarva_Shiksha_Abhiyan) program, which includes the introduction and improvement of mid-day school meals and the opening of new schools throughout India, especially in rural areas, to fight [illiteracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiteracy).[^cite_note-331] During Manmohan Singh's prime ministership, eight Institutes of Technology were opened in the states of Andhra Pradesh, Bihar, Gujarat, Orissa, Punjab, Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan, and Himachal Pradesh.[^cite_note-332]

### Foreign policies



The aligned countries on the northern hemisphere: [NATO](21133.md) in blue and the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) in red

refer caption

[Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser), [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru), and [Josip Broz Tito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josip_Broz_Tito), pioneers of the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement)

The Congress party played a foundational role in shaping India's non-aligned foreign policy during the [Cold War](325329.md), particularly under the leadership of Prime Minister [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru).[^cite_note-Non-Aligned-111][^cite_note-Russia_MFA-333] Nehru was one of the principal architects of the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) (NAM), which sought to maintain strategic autonomy by avoiding alignment with either the [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc) or [Soviet bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc).[^cite_note-j492-334] Throughout much of the Cold War period, Congress supported a foreign policy of non-alignment that called for India to form ties with both the Western and Eastern Blocs, but to avoid formal alliances with either.[^cite_note-Academic-335] However, in 1971, amid growing U.S. support for Pakistan—including military aid and diplomatic backing—the Congress-led Indian government signed [Indo-Soviet Treaty of Friendship and Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Soviet_Treaty_of_Friendship_and_Cooperation), marking a strategic shift toward the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-336]

Continuing the foreign policy initiated by P. V. Narasimha Rao, the Congress government pursued the [peace process with Pakistan](4500730.md), including high-level bilateral visits.[^cite_note-peace-337] The UPA government has tried to end the border dispute with the People's Republic of China through negotiations.[^cite_note-Economist_news-338][^cite_note-republic-339] [Relations with Afghanistan](17759586.md) have also been a concern for Congress.[^cite_note-340] During Afghan President [Hamid Karzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai)'s visit to New Delhi in August 2008, Manmohan Singh increased the aid package to Afghanistan for the development of schools, health clinics, infrastructure, and defence.[^cite_note-BBC2-341] India is now one of the single largest aid donors to Afghanistan.[^cite_note-BBC2-341] To nourish political, security, cultural and economical connections with central Asian countries, it launched [Connect Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connect_Central_Asia) policy in 2012. This policy is aimed at strengthening and expanding India's relations with [Kazakhstan](16642.md), [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md), [Tajikistan](30108.md), [Turkmenistan](198149.md), and [Uzbekistan](31853.md). [Look East policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Look_East_policy_\(India\)) was initiated in 1992 by [Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narsimha_Rao) to cultivate extensive economic and strategic relations with the nations of Southeast Asia to bolster its standing as a regional power and a counterweight to the strategic influence of the People's Republic of China. Subsequently, in 1992 Rao decided to bring into open India's relations with Israel, which had been kept covertly active for a few years during his tenure as a Foreign Minister, and permitted Israel to open an embassy in New Delhi.[^cite_note-IsraelRao-342] Rao decided to maintain a distance from the [Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalai_Lama) to avoid aggravating Beijing's suspicions and concerns, and made successful overtures to [Tehran](57654.md).[^cite_note-Irish1996-343]

Even though the Congress foreign policy doctrine stands for maintaining friendly relations with all the countries of the world, it has always exhibited a special bias towards the Afro-Asian nations. It played active role in forming [Group of 77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_of_77) (1964, [Group of 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_of_15) (1990), [Indian Ocean Rim Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_Rim_Association), and [SAARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAARC). Indira Gandhi firmly tied Indian anti-imperialist interests in Africa to those of the Soviet Union. She openly and enthusiastically supported liberation struggles in Africa.[^cite_note-MEAIndira-344] In April 2006, New Delhi hosted an India–Africa summit attended by the leaders of 15 African states.[^cite_note-345]

The party opposes arms race and advocates disarmament, both conventional and nuclear.[^cite_note-IndiaToday2000-346] When in power between 2004 and 2014, Congress worked on [India's relationship with the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_States_relations). Prime Minister Manmohan Singh visited the US in July 2005 to negotiate an [India–United States Civil Nuclear Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_States_Civil_Nuclear_Agreement). US president [George W. Bush](3414021.md) visited India in March 2006; during this visit, a nuclear agreement that would give India access to nuclear fuel and technology in exchange for the [IAEA](14984.md) inspection of its civil [nuclear reactors](22151.md) was proposed. Over two years of negotiations, followed by approval from the IAEA, the [Nuclear Suppliers Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Suppliers_Group) and the [United States Congress](31756.md), the agreement was signed on 10 October 2008.[^cite_note-347] However, it has not signed [Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Non-Proliferation_Treaty) (NPT) and [Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Nuclear-Test-Ban_Treaty) (CTBT) due to their discriminatory and hegemonistic nature.[^cite_note-NTI2019-348][^cite_note-Sipri2010-349]

Congress' policy has been to cultivate friendly relations with Japan as well as [European Union](9317.md) countries including the United Kingdom, France, and Germany.[^cite_note-conversation-350] Diplomatic relations with Iran have continued, and negotiations over the [Iran-Pakistan-India gas pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran-Pakistan-India_gas_pipeline) have taken place.[^cite_note-Iran_relations-351] Congress' policy has also been to improve relations with other developing countries, particularly Brazil and South Africa.[^cite_note-352] While in opposition, the leadership of the Indian National Congress backed the Indian government's stance to abstain from UN resolutions regarding the [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md).[^cite_note-353] In 2025, the Congress criticised Prime Minister [Narendra Modi](444222.md) for his stance on [Israel](9282173.md) and the [Gaza war](74998519.md).[^cite_note-354]

## Structure and composition

See also: [List of national presidents of the Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_presidents_of_the_Indian_National_Congress), [All India Congress Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Congress_Committee), and [Congress Working Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_Working_Committee)



Stamps commemorating centenary of the Congress

At present, the [president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_Indian_National_Congress) and the [All India Congress Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Congress_Committee) (AICC) are elected by delegates from state and district parties at an annual national conference; in every Indian state and union territory—or *pradesh*—there is a [Pradesh Congress Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pradesh_Congress_Committee) (PCC),[^cite_note-Committee-355] which is the state-level unit of the party responsible for directing political campaigns at local and state levels, and assisting the campaigns for parliamentary constituencies.[^cite_note-356] Each PCC has a working committee of twenty members, most of whom are appointed by the party president, the leader of the state party, who is chosen by the national president. Those elected as members of the states' legislative assemblies form the Congress Legislature Parties in the various state assemblies; their chairperson is usually the party's nominee for Chief Ministership. The party is also organised into various committees, and sections; it publishes a daily newspaper, the *[National Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_National_Herald_\(India\))*.[^cite_note-Kumar1990-357] Despite being a party with a structure, Congress under Indira Gandhi did not hold any organisational elections after 1972.[^cite_note-358] Nonetheless, in 2004, when the Congress was voted back into power, [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) became the first prime minister not to be the president of the party since establishment of the practice of the president holding both positions.[^cite_note-359]

The AICC is composed of delegates sent from the PCCs.[^cite_note-Kumar1990-357] The delegates elect Congress committees, including the [Congress Working Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_Working_Committee), consisting of senior party leaders and office-bearers. The AICC takes all-important executive and political decisions. Since Indira Gandhi formed Congress (I) in 1978, the [President of the Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_President) has effectively been the party's national leader, head of the organisation, head of the Working Committee and all chief Congress committees, chief spokesman, and Congress' choice for [Prime Minister of India](24452.md). Constitutionally, the president is elected by the PCCs and members of the AICC; however, this procedure has often been bypassed by the Working Committee, which has elected its candidate.[^cite_note-Kumar1990-357]

Congress student wing annual conference

&#x20;[National Students' Union of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Students'_Union_of_India) (NSUI) National Convention Inquilab-1 in Jaipur

The Congress Parliamentary Party (CPP) consists of elected MPs in the Lok Sabha and [Rajya Sabha](232210.md). There is also a Congress Legislative Party (CLP) leader in each state. The CLP consists of all Congress [Members of the Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Legislative_Assembly_\(India\)) (MLAs) in each state. In cases of states where the Congress is single-handedly ruling the government, the CLP leader in the [chief minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_minister). Other directly affiliated groups include:

- [National Students' Union of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Students'_Union_of_India) (NSUI), the students' wing of the Congress.

- [Indian Youth Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Youth_Congress), the party's youth wing.

- [Indian National Trade Union Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Trade_Union_Congress), the labour union.

- [All India Mahila Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Mahila_Congress), its women's division.

- [Kisan and Khet Mazdoor Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kisan_and_Khet_Mazdoor_Congress), its peasant's wing.

- [All India Professionals Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Professionals_Congress), its working professionals wing.

- Congress [Seva Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seva_Dal), its voluntary organisation.

  [^cite_note-360]

  [^cite_note-361]

- All India Congress Minority Department, also referred to as Minority Congress is the minority wing of the Congress party. It is represented by the *Pradesh Congress Minority Department* in all the [states of India](375986.md).

  [^cite_note-362]

### Election symbols

| Election symbol until 1969 | Approved symbol of Indira Gandhi CongressElection symbol during the period 1971–1977 | Election symbol since 1980 |
| --- | --- | --- |

As of 2021, the [election symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_symbol) of Congress, as approved by the [Election Commission of India](47863083.md), is an image of a right hand with its palm facing front and its fingers pressed together;[^cite_note-hand-363] this is usually shown in the centre of a tricolor flag. The hand symbol was first used by Indira Gandhi when she split from the Congress (R) faction following the 1977 elections and created the New Congress (I).[^cite_note-hand_symbol-364] The hand is symbolic of strength, energy, and unity.

The party under the stewardship of Nehru had the symbol 'Pair of bullocks carrying a yoke' which struck a chord with masses who were predominantly farmers.[^cite_note-bullocks-365] In 1969, due to internal conflicts within the Congress party, Indira Gandhi decided to break out and form a party of her own, with the majority of the Congress party members in support of her in the new party which was named Congress(R). The symbol of Indira's [Congress (R)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_\(R\)) or Congress (Requisitionists) during the 1971–1977 period was a cow with a suckling calf.[^cite_note-Electoral_Symbol-366][^cite_note-book-146] After losing the support of 76 out of the party's 153 members in the Lok Sabha, Indira's new political outfit the Congress (I) or Congress (Indira) evolved and she opted for the hand (open palm) symbol.

### Dynasticism

Dynasticism is fairly common in many [political parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_India) in India, including the Congress party.[^cite_note-Denyer2014-367] Six members of the [Nehru–Gandhi family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nehru%E2%80%93Gandhi_family) have been presidents of the party.[^cite_note-Gandhi_presidents-368] The party started being controlled by Indira Gandhi's family during [the emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Emergency_\(India\)) with her younger son, [Sanjay Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjay_Gandhi), taking on a prominent role.[^cite_note-Tarlo2003-369] This was characterised by servility and sycophancy towards the family which later led to a hereditary succession of Rajiv Gandhi as successor after Indira Gandhi's assassination, as well as the party's selection of Sonia Gandhi as Rajiv's successor after his assassination, which she turned down.[^cite_note-Bose2013-370] Since the formation of Congress (I) by Indira Gandhi in 1978 till 2022, the party president has been from her family except for the period between 1991 and 1998. In the last three elections to the Lok Sabha combined, 37 per cent of Congress party MPs had family members precede them in politics.[^cite_note-Chandra2016-371] However, in recent times there have been calls from within the party to restructure the organisation. A group of senior leaders wrote a letter to the party president to reform the Congress allowing others to take charge. There was also visible discontent following the loss in the [2019 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indian_general_election) after which a [group of 23 senior leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-23_\(political_group\)) wrote to the Congress President to restructure the party.[^cite_note-372]

## Presence in states and Union Territories

Indian National Congress is the only political party in India, which has experience of running the state government of every Indian states in post-independence Indian history. From the first general election in 1952 when Jawaharlal Nehru led it to a landslide victory, the Congress won in the majority of the following state elections and paved the way for a Nehruvian era of single-party dominance. The party during the post-independence era has governed most of the [States and union territories of India](375986.md).[^cite_note-Dominance-373] As of May 2026, the INC is in power in the states of [Telangana](990267.md), [Himachal Pradesh](14190.md), [Karnataka](16880.md) and [Kerala](4349459.md). In [Jharkhand](197225.md), it shares power as a junior ally with [Jharkhand Mukti Morcha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jharkhand_Mukti_Morcha).[^cite_note-JH_Alliance-374] In [Tamil Nadu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_Nadu_Legislative_Assembly) its a junior ally of the [TVK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamilaga_Vettri_Kazhagam) and in [Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md) with [JKNC](512306.md). The Congress has previously been the sole party in power in Delhi, Andhra Pradesh, [Meghalaya](326019.md), Haryana, [Uttarakhand](1429154.md) and in the [Union Territory](375986.md) of [Puducherry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puducherry_\(union_territory\)). Congress has enjoyed overwhelming electoral majority for over decades in [Arunachal Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arunachal_Pradesh), Delhi, Kerala, [Maharashtra](20629.md) and [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_India). It has a regional political alliance in Bihar, which is named *[Mahagathbandhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahagathbandhan_\(Bihar\))*, in Tamil Nadu it is the *[Secular Social Justice Victory Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_Social_Justice_Victory_Alliance)*, in Jharkhand, it is also the *[Mahagathbandhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahagathbandhan_\(Jharkhand\))*, in Maharashtra, it is the *[Maha Vikas Aghadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maha_Vikas_Aghadi),* and in Kerala, it is the *[United Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Democratic_Front_\(Kerala\)).*[^cite_note-TN_Alliance-375][^cite_note-KL_Alliance-376]

Retired from office upon completion of tenure on 11 August 2017, becoming the first vice president to serve under three presidents. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

## Legislative leaders

The Congress party has played a pivotal role in shaping India's political landscape, with prominent leaders such as [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru), [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi), and [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) as serving the country's longest-serving [prime ministers](24452.md) and contributing significantly to the nation's development. [Gulzarilal Nanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulzarilal_Nanda) took office in 1966 following the death of [Lal Bahadur Shastri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lal_Bahadur_Shastri) for 13 days as the [acting Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acting_Prime_Minister) of India.[^cite_note-385] His earlier 13-day stint as the second Prime Minister of India followed the death of Prime Minister Jawaharlal Nehru in 1964. Indira Gandhi, the first and so far the only woman Prime Minister of India, served the second-longest term as a prime minister.[^cite_note-indira-386] [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi) served from 1984 to 1989, taking office on the day of the [assassination of Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Indira_Gandhi) in 1984. At age 40, he was the youngest PM of India and was known for economic reforms during his tenure. [PV Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PV_Narasimha_Rao) served as the 10th Prime Minister of India and was the first PM from [southern India](678583.md).[^cite_note-Rao-387] [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) served two complete terms as Prime Minister and headed the coalition governments twice.

## Further reading

- *The Indian National Congress: An Historical Sketch*, by Frederick Marion De Mello. Published by H. Milford, Oxford University Press, 1934.

- *The Indian National Congress*, by Hemendra Nath Das Gupta. Published by J. K. Das Gupta, 1946.

- *Indian National Congress: A Descriptive Bibliography of India's Struggle for Freedom*, by Jagdish Saran Sharma. Published by S. Chand, 1959.

- *Social Factors in the Birth and Growth of the Indian National Congress Movement*, by Ramparkash Dua. Published by S. Chand, 1967.

- *Split in a Predominant Party: The Indian National Congress in 1969*, by Mahendra Prasad Singh. Abhinav Publications, 1981. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [81-7017-140-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/81-7017-140-7).

- *Concise History of the Indian National Congress, 1885–1947*, by B. N. Pande, Nisith Ranjan Ray, Ravinder Kumar, Manmath Nath Das. Published by Vikas Pub. House, 1985. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-7069-3020-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-7069-3020-7).

- *The Indian National Congress: An Analytical Biography*, by Om P. Gautam. Published by B.R. Pub. Corp., 1985.

- *A Century of Indian National Congress, 1885–1985*, by Pran Nath Chopra, Ram Gopal, Moti Lal Bhargava. Published by Agam Prakashan, 1986.

- *The Congress Ideology and Programme, 1920–1985*, by Pitambar Datt Kaushik. Published by Gitanjali Pub. House, 1986. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [81-85060-16-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/81-85060-16-9).

- *Struggling and Ruling: The Indian National Congress, 1885–1985*, by [Jim Masselos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Masselos). Published by Sterling Publishers, 1987.

- *The Encyclopedia of Indian National Congress*, by A. Moin Zaidi, Shaheda Gufran Zaidi, Indian Institute of Applied Political Research. Published by S.Chand, 1987.

- *Indian National Congress: A Reconstruction*, by Iqbal Singh, Nehru Memorial Museum and Library. Published by Riverdale Company, 1988. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-913215-32-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-913215-32-5).

- *INC, the Glorious Tradition*, by A. Moin Zaidi, Indian National Congress. AICC. Published by Indian Institute of Applied Political Research, 1989.

- *Indian National Congress: A Select Bibliography*, by Manikrao Hodlya Gavit, Attar Chand. Published by U.D.H. Pub. House, 1989. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [81-85044-05-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/81-85044-05-8).

- *The Story of Congress PilgrFile: 1885–1985*, by A. Moin Zaidi, Indian National Congress. Published by Indian Institute of Applied Political Research, 1990. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [81-85355-46-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/81-85355-46-0). (7 vols)

- *Indian National Congress in England*, by Harish P. Kaushik. Published by Friends Publications, 1991.

- *Women in Indian National Congress, 1921–1931*, by Rajan Mahan. Published by Rawat Publications, 1999.

- *History of Indian National Congress, 1885–2002*, by Deep Chand Bandhu. Published by Kalpaz Publications, 2003. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [81-7835-090-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/81-7835-090-4).

- [Bipan Chandra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipan_Chandra), Amales Tripathi, Barun De. *Freedom Struggle*. India: National Book Struggle. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-81-237-0249-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-81-237-0249-0).
